I’ll be going along normally enough, and then something will catch my attention and I’ll start wondering if there’s more meaning in it than there should be. Some days it’s barely there, and other days I can lose quite a lot of time turning it over in my head.\n\nAnd the voice… that’s not all the time either. It happens sometimes, and sometimes I answer it, which I know sounds strange when I say it out loud. But again, it’s been part of this same stretch of time — roughly four months — not something that’s been lifelong or happening every single moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble putting your thoughts into words when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: Not exactly trouble finding the words, no. I can usually put a sentence together — sometimes too many sentences, honestly. I know I go on and explain around things, especially if I’m worried I haven’t been clear.\n\nBut I have noticed something different, which may be related or may not be what you’re asking. For many months now, it’s felt like the… the warmth has gone out of me a bit. Not that I don’t care. I do care about my students, and I care about my friends. If one of them were upset or needed something, I’d still feel responsible, I’d still want them to be all right. But the drive to reach out, to take part, to keep things going socially — that has really faded.\n\nI used to be the sort of person who would help organize things, like book club, or planning a theater outing, or just ringing someone because I hadn’t heard from them. And now I let those things slide unless someone gives me a nudge or practically puts it in front of me. Even simple phone calls feel like something I keep postponing, not because I dislike the person, but because I can’t seem to summon the energy or the feeling behind it.\n\nSo with speaking, if I’m already talking to someone, the words are there eventually. But starting the conversation, keeping up that ordinary back-and-forth, showing the same warmth I used to — that’s harder than it was. It makes me feel guilty, because from the outside it probably looks like I’ve become indifferent, and I don’t think that’s true. I’m a secondary school teacher, and lately I just can’t shake the feeling that some of the staff emails, and even the morning announcements, are… not exactly what they seem. Like they’re coded messages about me.\n\nI’ll read an email that’s been sent to everyone, or hear an announcement over the speakers, and there’ll be a phrase or a timing or just the way something is worded, and it feels very pointed, like it’s referring to me or something I’ve done. My colleagues have been kind about it — they’ll show me, “Look, this went to the whole staff,” or explain that the announcement was general — and I can see that, I really can. But the feeling doesn’t go away easily. It stays with me, and I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nI came because it’s starting to bother me, I guess. I don’t want to keep misreading things at work, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that others can't hear, commenting on you or your actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve been hesitant to say that because it sounds worse when I put it into words. It’s more when things are quiet — like at home in the evening, when the house has settled, or sometimes after the classroom empties and there’s that strange silence after a whole day of noise. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, quite clearly. Not like a vague thought, more like someone has just spoken from nearby. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I can be long-winded — my students would probably tell you I take the scenic route to get to a point, and my colleagues sometimes tease me that I give too much background before answering a simple question. But that’s not new, really. I’ve always been a bit like that, especially when I’m trying to be precise or explain something properly.\n\nAs far as losing the thread completely, or people not understanding me… I don’t think so. At school I can still teach a lesson and follow what I’m saying. I might circle around a point if I’m anxious or if I’m trying to make sense of something, but I wouldn’t say my thoughts are impossible to express. At least, I haven’t had anyone tell me that I’m not making sense.\n\nUnless you mean something more subtle? Sometimes when I’m under pressure I feel like there are too many thoughts at once, and I have to slow myself down, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities that seem unusual or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it’s out of character enough that I’ve noticed it, and then I feel foolish afterward.\n\nFor example, during lessons, I’ll be in the middle of doing something perfectly ordinary — handing out worksheets, setting up an activity, explaining something on the board — and I’ll suddenly get caught on the markers or the papers on my desk. I’ll stop and start arranging them in a very particular way. Not just tidying, I mean more like… the blue marker has to be beside the green one, the papers have to be squared off, or lined up in a certain pattern, and I’ll spend too long on it while the students are waiting. Like they’ll say, “We need to keep it simple,” and I’ll say, “Keep it simple, yes, keep it simple,” almost automatically, rather than giving a proper response. And then there’s this awkward pause, because I know it sounds strange, and I feel embarrassed. I usually try to cover it by turning it into a joke or pretending I was thinking aloud, but it doesn’t feel quite normal for me.\n\nIt’s the interruption that bothers me most. I can still do my work, but these little moments pull me off track, and I’m aware afterward that it wasn’t how I would usually behave.\n\nDoctor: How long do these feelings of coded messages and hearing voices typically last after they start?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Not every day, and not always in the same way, which makes it harder to pin down neatly, but if I’m being honest, that’s roughly when I first started noticing both things — the feeling that certain things were sort of coded or meant for me, and the voice that I sometimes respond to before I realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just a bad week, or being overtired, or my mind making patterns because I was stressed. So I didn’t mark it down like, “This began on this date.” But looking back, it’s been around four months that it’s been happening enough for me to feel unsettled by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often fragmented or difficult to put into words when these feelings of coded messages or hearing voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as fragmented, exactly. When it’s happening, it’s more that I become very preoccupied, if that makes sense. My attention gets pulled toward whatever it is, and then I’m trying to work out what it means or whether I’m imagining it, and that can make it harder to speak smoothly.\n\nBut I don’t think my thoughts are in pieces, or that I can’t put words together. I can usually explain myself if I slow down. It’s more like there’s an extra layer going on in my head while I’m trying to talk, and that makes me hesitate or go around the point too much. I might pause, or repeat myself, or lose confidence in what I was saying for a moment.\n\nSo… difficult, yes, in the sense of being distracted or unsure. But not completely jumbled, I don’t think. At least, I haven’t experienced it as my mind breaking apart into disconnected bits.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to a topic when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way, anyway. I mean, I can be a bit long-winded — my colleagues would probably say I explain things with too much background, especially when I’m trying to be precise — but that’s not new for me. I’ve always been the sort of person who gives the context before the answer.\n\nSometimes I do notice myself circling around a point more than I’d like, or taking a detour because I’m trying to make sure I’m being understood properly. But if someone says, “Sorry, what were you saying?” I can usually get back to the topic. I don’t feel as though I’m constantly jumping from subject to subject without realizing it.\n\nSo… I suppose I may wander a little when I’m anxious or trying to explain something complicated, but I wouldn’t say I often have trouble sticking to the topic in conversation.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences, such as hearing voices or feeling like messages are coded, caused problems in your relationships with colleagues, students, or family members?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, like everything has fallen apart, but yes, it has caused problems. I’m still teaching, I should say that first — I haven’t stopped working, and I’m still getting through my classes. But I’m not functioning quite the way I normally would.\n\nWith colleagues, I’ve had to lean on people more than I like. There have been project lessons where I just couldn’t manage all the moving parts properly, and coworkers had to cover parts of it or step in more than they usually would. And in planning meetings, I sometimes need help getting through the agenda or keeping things organized enough to be useful. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been someone people could depend on for that kind of thing.\n\nWith students, I try very hard not to let it show. I don’t think I’ve been… harmful or anything like that. But I know I’m less steady than I used to be, especially with the more open-ended lessons where there are lots of materials, instructions, and questions all happening at once.\n\nAnd outside school, I’ve become unreliable. I’ve missed book club, theater plans, neighborhood things — not because I don’t care, but because I keep pulling back or cancelling, or I just don’t have the confidence that I’ll be able to be present and normal through the whole thing. So yes, it’s affected relationships, mostly by making me less dependable than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that don't make sense to you or seem disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… or at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. My thoughts can feel crowded, and I can get stuck trying to interpret something or worrying about whether I’ve understood it properly, but they don’t usually feel completely disconnected from each other.\n\nIt’s more like I follow a thread too far, if that makes sense. One idea leads to another and then another, and by the time I’m explaining it out loud it may sound overcomplicated, because I’m giving all the steps in between. But in my own head there’s usually a reason I got from one point to the next. It may not be a good reason, or it may seem excessive when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel random to me.\n\nSometimes afterward I might think, “Why did I spend so long on that?” or “That probably didn’t need five minutes of explanation.” But I wouldn’t say I’m having thoughts that don’t make sense to me, exactly. More that I get tangled in them, or I overthink them, and then I have trouble being concise.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having trouble understanding your own thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. It’s not like I’m sitting there unable to name whether I’m sad or angry or frightened.\n\nWhere I do get muddled is more in… sorting out why I’m feeling that way, or how much weight to give it. For example, I might feel uneasy and then spend too long trying to work out whether there’s a real reason for it, or whether I’m just overreacting, or whether I’ve misunderstood something. So the feeling itself is recognizable, but then I start analyzing it and making it more complicated than it probably needs to be.\n\nWith thoughts, it’s similar. But the drive to reach out, to take part, to keep things going socially — that has really faded.\n\nI used to be the sort of person who would help organize things, like book club, or planning a theater outing, or just ringing someone because I hadn’t heard from them. And now I let those things slide unless someone gives me a nudge or practically puts it in front of me. Even simple phone calls feel like something I keep postponing, not because I dislike the person, but because I can’t seem to summon the energy or the feeling behind it.\n\nSo with speaking, if I’m already talking to someone, the words are there eventually. But starting the conversation, keeping up that ordinary back-and-forth, showing the same warmth I used to — that’s harder than it was. It makes me feel guilty, because from the outside it probably looks like I’ve become indifferent, and I don’t think that’s true. It’s more like something has drained out of me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself rambling or having trouble staying on topic when speaking to others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I ramble in the sense of not making sense or completely losing the thread. I mean… I’m aware I can be long-winded. You’ve probably noticed that already. I tend to explain the background, then the background to the background, because I don’t want to give the wrong impression or leave out something important.

But if you asked me whether I’m unaware that I’ve gone off topic, I don’t think so. Usually I know what point I’m trying to get to, even if I take a rather scenic route to get there. Sometimes people look a bit impatient, and then I realize I’ve been circling something instead of just saying it plainly. That’s embarrassing, especially at work, because I’m used to needing to be clear.

So yes, I can talk too much around a subject, particularly when I’m nervous or trying to be precise. But I wouldn’t say I often lose the topic entirely. It’s more that I over-explain.

---------- turn 13 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F28\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brought me in is something that’s been happening at school, and I know it may sound a bit odd when I say it out loud. I’m a secondary school teacher, and lately I just can’t shake the feeling that some of the staff emails, and even the morning announcements, are… not exactly what they seem. Like they’re coded messages about me.\n\nI’ll read an email that’s been sent to everyone, or hear an announcement over the speakers, and there’ll be a phrase or a timing or just the way something is worded, and it feels very pointed, like it’s referring to me or something I’ve done. My colleagues have been kind about it — they’ll show me, “Look, this went to the whole staff,” or explain that the announcement was general — and I can see that, I really can. But the feeling doesn’t go away easily. It stays with me, and I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nI came because it’s starting to bother me, I guess. I don’t want to keep misreading things at work, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that others can't hear, commenting on you or your actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve been hesitant to say that because it sounds worse when I put it into words. But yes, sometimes I do.\n\nIt’s not constant or anything. It’s more when things are quiet — like at home in the evening, when the house has settled, or sometimes after the classroom empties and there’s that strange silence after a whole day of noise. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, quite clearly. Not like a vague thought, more like someone has just spoken from nearby. And sometimes it’ll be a short comment about what I’m doing, like just a few words, nothing long.\n\nA couple of times I’ve actually answered before I caught myself, which was embarrassing even though no one was there to see it. I’ll say, “Yes?” or turn around expecting someone to be standing behind me, and then I realize the room is empty. It unsettles me because in the moment it really does sound external, not like my own inner voice. Then afterward I try to explain it away — tiredness, stress, the acoustics of the building, something from the corridor — but it’s clear enough that I can’t always dismiss it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble keeping your conversations on track?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I can be long-winded — my students would probably tell you I take the scenic route to get to a point, and my colleagues sometimes tease me that I give too much background before answering a simple question. But that’s not new, really. I’ve always been a bit like that, especially when I’m trying to be precise or explain something properly.\n\nAs far as losing the thread completely, or people not understanding me… I don’t think so. At school I can still teach a lesson and follow what I’m saying. I might circle around a point if I’m anxious or if I’m trying to make sense of something, but I wouldn’t say my thoughts are impossible to express. At least, I haven’t had anyone tell me that I’m not making sense.\n\nUnless you mean something more subtle? Sometimes when I’m under pressure I feel like there are too many thoughts at once, and I have to slow myself down, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities that seem unusual or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it’s out of character enough that I’ve noticed it, and then I feel foolish afterward.\n\nFor example, during lessons, I’ll be in the middle of doing something perfectly ordinary — handing out worksheets, setting up an activity, explaining something on the board — and I’ll suddenly get caught on the markers or the papers on my desk. I’ll stop and start arranging them in a very particular way. Not just tidying, I mean more like… the blue marker has to be beside the green one, the papers have to be squared off, or lined up in a certain pattern, and I’ll spend too long on it while the students are waiting. Then I sort of come back to myself and realize I’ve interrupted the flow of the lesson.\n\nIt’s happened in art-fair meetings too. We’ll be discussing displays or budgets or where things should go, and instead of just answering someone, I might repeat a phrase they’ve just said. Like they’ll say, “We need to keep it simple,” and I’ll say, “Keep it simple, yes, keep it simple,” almost automatically, rather than giving a proper response. And then there’s this awkward pause, because I know it sounds strange, and I feel embarrassed. I usually try to cover it by turning it into a joke or pretending I was thinking aloud, but it doesn’t feel quite normal for me.\n\nIt’s the interruption that bothers me most. I can still do my work, but these little moments pull me off track, and I’m aware afterward that it wasn’t how I would usually behave.\n\nDoctor: How long do these feelings of coded messages and hearing voices typically last after they start?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Not every day, and not always in the same way, which makes it harder to pin down neatly, but if I’m being honest, that’s roughly when I first started noticing both things — the feeling that certain things were sort of coded or meant for me, and the voice that I sometimes respond to before I realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just a bad week, or being overtired, or my mind making patterns because I was stressed. So I didn’t mark it down like, “This began on this date.” But looking back, it’s been around four months that it’s been happening enough for me to feel unsettled by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often fragmented or difficult to put into words when these feelings of coded messages or hearing voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as fragmented, exactly. When it’s happening, it’s more that I become very preoccupied, if that makes sense. My attention gets pulled toward whatever it is, and then I’m trying to work out what it means or whether I’m imagining it, and that can make it harder to speak smoothly.\n\nBut I don’t think my thoughts are in pieces, or that I can’t put words together. I can usually explain myself if I slow down. It’s more like there’s an extra layer going on in my head while I’m trying to talk, and that makes me hesitate or go around the point too much. I might pause, or repeat myself, or lose confidence in what I was saying for a moment.\n\nSo… difficult, yes, in the sense of being distracted or unsure. But not completely jumbled, I don’t think. At least, I haven’t experienced it as my mind breaking apart into disconnected bits.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to a topic when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way, anyway. I mean, I can be a bit long-winded — my colleagues would probably say I explain things with too much background, especially when I’m trying to be precise — but that’s not new for me. I’ve always been the sort of person who gives the context before the answer.\n\nSometimes I do notice myself circling around a point more than I’d like, or taking a detour because I’m trying to make sure I’m being understood properly. But if someone says, “Sorry, what were you saying?” I can usually get back to the topic. I don’t feel as though I’m constantly jumping from subject to subject without realizing it.\n\nSo… I suppose I may wander a little when I’m anxious or trying to explain something complicated, but I wouldn’t say I often have trouble sticking to the topic in conversation.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences, such as hearing voices or feeling like messages are coded, caused problems in your relationships with colleagues, students, or family members?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, like everything has fallen apart, but yes, it has caused problems. I’m still teaching, I should say that first — I haven’t stopped working, and I’m still getting through my classes. But I’m not functioning quite the way I normally would.\n\nWith colleagues, I’ve had to lean on people more than I like. There have been project lessons where I just couldn’t manage all the moving parts properly, and coworkers had to cover parts of it or step in more than they usually would. And in planning meetings, I sometimes need help getting through the agenda or keeping things organized enough to be useful. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been someone people could depend on for that kind of thing.\n\nWith students, I try very hard not to let it show. I don’t think I’ve been… harmful or anything like that. But I know I’m less steady than I used to be, especially with the more open-ended lessons where there are lots of materials, instructions, and questions all happening at once.\n\nAnd outside school, I’ve become unreliable. I’ve missed book club, theater plans, neighborhood things — not because I don’t care, but because I keep pulling back or cancelling, or I just don’t have the confidence that I’ll be able to be present and normal through the whole thing. So yes, it’s affected relationships, mostly by making me less dependable than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that don't make sense to you or seem disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… or at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. My thoughts can feel crowded, and I can get stuck trying to interpret something or worrying about whether I’ve understood it properly, but they don’t usually feel completely disconnected from each other.\n\nIt’s more like I follow a thread too far, if that makes sense. One idea leads to another and then another, and by the time I’m explaining it out loud it may sound overcomplicated, because I’m giving all the steps in between. But in my own head there’s usually a reason I got from one point to the next. It may not be a good reason, or it may seem excessive when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel random to me.\n\nSometimes afterward I might think, “Why did I spend so long on that?” or “That probably didn’t need five minutes of explanation.” But I wouldn’t say I’m having thoughts that don’t make sense to me, exactly. More that I get tangled in them, or I overthink them, and then I have trouble being concise.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having trouble understanding your own thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I understand what I’m feeling most of the time — anxious, embarrassed, tired, frustrated with myself — those things are fairly clear to me. It’s not like I’m sitting there unable to name whether I’m sad or angry or frightened.\n\nWhere I do get muddled is more in… sorting out why I’m feeling that way, or how much weight to give it. For example, I might feel uneasy and then spend too long trying to work out whether there’s a real reason for it, or whether I’m just overreacting, or whether I’ve misunderstood something. So the feeling itself is recognizable, but then I start analyzing it and making it more complicated than it probably needs to be.\n\nWith thoughts, it’s similar. I don’t usually feel as if my own thoughts are impossible to understand. They can be busy, and I can get tangled up in them, but I can generally follow what I mean. Sometimes I wish I could step back from them more cleanly, because I’ll go round and round trying to explain something to myself. But I wouldn’t say I often don’t understand my own thoughts or feelings. More that I over-examine them until they feel less clear than they were at the start.\n\nDoctor: Do these experiences, such as hearing voices or feeling like messages are coded, happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It isn’t constant, like every minute of the day, but it has been going on for a while now — about four months, I think, if I’m being honest about the timing.\n\nThe coded-message feeling is the one that tends to come up in patches. I’ll be going along normally enough, and then something will catch my attention and I’ll start wondering if there’s more meaning in it than there should be. Some days it’s barely there, and other days I can lose quite a lot of time turning it over in my head.\n\nAnd the voice… that’s not all the time either. It happens sometimes, and sometimes I answer it, which I know sounds strange when I say it out loud. But again, it’s been part of this same stretch of time — roughly four months — not something that’s been lifelong or happening every single moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble putting your thoughts into words when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: Not exactly trouble finding the words, no. I can usually put a sentence together — sometimes too many sentences, honestly. I know I go on and explain around things, especially if I’m worried I haven’t been clear.\n\nBut I have noticed something different, which may be related or may not be what you’re asking. For many months now, it’s felt like the… the warmth has gone out of me a bit. Not that I don’t care. I do care about my students, and I care about my friends. If one of them were upset or needed something, I’d still feel responsible, I’d still want them to be all right. But the drive to reach out, to take part, to keep things going socially — that has really faded.\n\nI used to be the sort of person who would help organize things, like book club, or planning a theater outing, or just ringing someone because I hadn’t heard from them. And now I let those things slide unless someone gives me a nudge or practically puts it in front of me. Even simple phone calls feel like something I keep postponing, not because I dislike the person, but because I can’t seem to summon the energy or the feeling behind it.\n\nSo with speaking, if I’m already talking to someone, the words are there eventually. But starting the conversation, keeping up that ordinary back-and-forth, showing the same warmth I used to — that’s harder than it was. It makes me feel guilty, because from the outside it probably looks like I’ve become indifferent, and I don’t think that’s true. It’s more like something has drained out of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself rambling or having trouble staying on topic when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I ramble in the sense of not making sense or completely losing the thread. I mean… I’m aware I can be long-winded. You’ve probably noticed that already. I tend to explain the background, then the background to the background, because I don’t want to give the wrong impression or leave out something important.\n\nBut if you asked me whether I’m unaware that I’ve gone off topic, I don’t think so. Usually I know what point I’m trying to get to, even if I take a rather scenic route to get there. Sometimes people look a bit impatient, and then I realize I’ve been circling something instead of just saying it plainly. That’s embarrassing, especially at work, because I’m used to needing to be clear.\n\nSo yes, I can talk too much around a subject, particularly when I’m nervous or trying to be precise. But I wouldn’t say I often lose the topic entirely. It’s more that I over-explain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F23", "F28"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

It may not be a good reason, or it may seem excessive when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel random to me.\n\nSometimes afterward I might think, “Why did I spend so long on that?” or “That probably didn’t need five minutes of explanation.” But I wouldn’t say I’m having thoughts that don’t make sense to me, exactly. More that I get tangled in them, or I overthink them, and then I have trouble being concise.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having trouble understanding your own thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I understand what I’m feeling most of the time — anxious, embarrassed, tired, frustrated with myself — those things are fairly clear to me. It’s not like I’m sitting there unable to name whether I’m sad or angry or frightened.\n\nWhere I do get muddled is more in… sorting out why I’m feeling that way, or how much weight to give it. For example, I might feel uneasy and then spend too long trying to work out whether there’s a real reason for it, or whether I’m just overreacting, or whether I’ve misunderstood something. So the feeling itself is recognizable, but then I start analyzing it and making it more complicated than it probably needs to be.\n\nWith thoughts, it’s similar. I don’t usually feel as if my own thoughts are impossible to understand. They can be busy, and I can get tangled up in them, but I can generally follow what I mean. Sometimes I wish I could step back from them more cleanly, because I’ll go round and round trying to explain something to myself. But I wouldn’t say I often don’t understand my own thoughts or feelings. More that I over-examine them until they feel less clear than they were at the start.\n\nDoctor: Do these experiences, such as hearing voices or feeling like messages are coded, happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It isn’t constant, like every minute of the day, but it has been going on for a while now — about four months, I think, if I’m being honest about the timing.\n\nThe coded-message feeling is the one that tends to come up in patches. I’ll be going along normally enough, and then something will catch my attention and I’ll start wondering if there’s more meaning in it than there should be. Some days it’s barely there, and other days I can lose quite a lot of time turning it over in my head.\n\nAnd the voice… that’s not all the time either. It happens sometimes, and sometimes I answer it, which I know sounds strange when I say it out loud. But again, it’s been part of this same stretch of time — roughly four months — not something that’s been lifelong or happening every single moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble putting your thoughts into words when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: Not exactly trouble finding the words, no. I can usually put a sentence together — sometimes too many sentences, honestly. I know I go on and explain around things, especially if I’m worried I haven’t been clear.\n\nBut I have noticed something different, which may be related or may not be what you’re asking. For many months now, it’s felt like the… the warmth has gone out of me a bit. Not that I don’t care. I do care about my students, and I care about my friends. If one of them were upset or needed something, I’d still feel responsible, I’d still want them to be all right. But the drive to reach out, to take part, to keep things going socially — that has really faded.\n\nI used to be the sort of person who would help organize things, like book club, or planning a theater outing, or just ringing someone because I hadn’t heard from them. And now I let those things slide unless someone gives me a nudge or practically puts it in front of me. Even simple phone calls feel like something I keep postponing, not because I dislike the person, but because I can’t seem to summon the energy or the feeling behind it.\n\nSo with speaking, if I’m already talking to someone, the words are there eventually. But starting the conversation, keeping up that ordinary back-and-forth, showing the same warmth I used to — that’s harder than it was. It makes me feel guilty, because from the outside it probably looks like I’ve become indifferent, and I don’t think that’s true. It’s more like something has drained out of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself rambling or having trouble staying on topic when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I ramble in the sense of not making sense or completely losing the thread. I mean… I’m aware I can be long-winded. You’ve probably noticed that already. I tend to explain the background, then the background to the background, because I don’t want to give the wrong impression or leave out something important.\n\nBut if you asked me whether I’m unaware that I’ve gone off topic, I don’t think so. Usually I know what point I’m trying to get to, even if I take a rather scenic route to get there. Sometimes people look a bit impatient, and then I realize I’ve been circling something instead of just saying it plainly. That’s embarrassing, especially at work, because I’m used to needing to be clear.\n\nSo yes, I can talk too much around a subject, particularly when I’m nervous or trying to be precise. But I wouldn’t say I often lose the topic entirely. It’s more that I over-explain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think so, not often. At least, people don’t usually say they can’t understand me. If anything, I sometimes worry I’m saying too much, or taking too long to get to the point, but I usually feel that the point is still there somewhere.

With my students, for example, I’m used to checking whether I’m being clear, because that’s part of teaching — you can see it in their faces when you’ve lost them. And I wouldn’t say that’s been a major problem in the sense of my words not making sense. I might explain something in two or three different ways when one would have done, but that’s not quite the same as being unintelligible.

In conversations with friends, I suppose they might get tired or want me to be more direct, but I don’t think they’re confused by what I mean. Or if they are, they haven’t told me plainly. I’m more self-conscious about going on too long than about not being understood.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brought me in is something that’s been happening at school, and I know it may sound a bit odd when I say it out loud. I’m a secondary school teacher, and lately I just can’t shake the feeling that some of the staff emails, and even the morning announcements, are… not exactly what they seem. Like they’re coded messages about me.\n\nI’ll read an email that’s been sent to everyone, or hear an announcement over the speakers, and there’ll be a phrase or a timing or just the way something is worded, and it feels very pointed, like it’s referring to me or something I’ve done. My colleagues have been kind about it — they’ll show me, “Look, this went to the whole staff,” or explain that the announcement was general — and I can see that, I really can. But the feeling doesn’t go away easily. It stays with me, and I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nI came because it’s starting to bother me, I guess. I don’t want to keep misreading things at work, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that others can't hear, commenting on you or your actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve been hesitant to say that because it sounds worse when I put it into words. But yes, sometimes I do.\n\nIt’s not constant or anything. It’s more when things are quiet — like at home in the evening, when the house has settled, or sometimes after the classroom empties and there’s that strange silence after a whole day of noise. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, quite clearly. Not like a vague thought, more like someone has just spoken from nearby. And sometimes it’ll be a short comment about what I’m doing, like just a few words, nothing long.\n\nA couple of times I’ve actually answered before I caught myself, which was embarrassing even though no one was there to see it. I’ll say, “Yes?” or turn around expecting someone to be standing behind me, and then I realize the room is empty. It unsettles me because in the moment it really does sound external, not like my own inner voice. Then afterward I try to explain it away — tiredness, stress, the acoustics of the building, something from the corridor — but it’s clear enough that I can’t always dismiss it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble keeping your conversations on track?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I can be long-winded — my students would probably tell you I take the scenic route to get to a point, and my colleagues sometimes tease me that I give too much background before answering a simple question. But that’s not new, really. I’ve always been a bit like that, especially when I’m trying to be precise or explain something properly.\n\nAs far as losing the thread completely, or people not understanding me… I don’t think so. At school I can still teach a lesson and follow what I’m saying. I might circle around a point if I’m anxious or if I’m trying to make sense of something, but I wouldn’t say my thoughts are impossible to express. At least, I haven’t had anyone tell me that I’m not making sense.\n\nUnless you mean something more subtle? Sometimes when I’m under pressure I feel like there are too many thoughts at once, and I have to slow myself down, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities that seem unusual or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it’s out of character enough that I’ve noticed it, and then I feel foolish afterward.\n\nFor example, during lessons, I’ll be in the middle of doing something perfectly ordinary — handing out worksheets, setting up an activity, explaining something on the board — and I’ll suddenly get caught on the markers or the papers on my desk. I’ll stop and start arranging them in a very particular way. Not just tidying, I mean more like… the blue marker has to be beside the green one, the papers have to be squared off, or lined up in a certain pattern, and I’ll spend too long on it while the students are waiting. Then I sort of come back to myself and realize I’ve interrupted the flow of the lesson.\n\nIt’s happened in art-fair meetings too. We’ll be discussing displays or budgets or where things should go, and instead of just answering someone, I might repeat a phrase they’ve just said. Like they’ll say, “We need to keep it simple,” and I’ll say, “Keep it simple, yes, keep it simple,” almost automatically, rather than giving a proper response. And then there’s this awkward pause, because I know it sounds strange, and I feel embarrassed. I usually try to cover it by turning it into a joke or pretending I was thinking aloud, but it doesn’t feel quite normal for me.\n\nIt’s the interruption that bothers me most. I can still do my work, but these little moments pull me off track, and I’m aware afterward that it wasn’t how I would usually behave.\n\nDoctor: How long do these feelings of coded messages and hearing voices typically last after they start?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Not every day, and not always in the same way, which makes it harder to pin down neatly, but if I’m being honest, that’s roughly when I first started noticing both things — the feeling that certain things were sort of coded or meant for me, and the voice that I sometimes respond to before I realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just a bad week, or being overtired, or my mind making patterns because I was stressed. So I didn’t mark it down like, “This began on this date.” But looking back, it’s been around four months that it’s been happening enough for me to feel unsettled by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often fragmented or difficult to put into words when these feelings of coded messages or hearing voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as fragmented, exactly. When it’s happening, it’s more that I become very preoccupied, if that makes sense. My attention gets pulled toward whatever it is, and then I’m trying to work out what it means or whether I’m imagining it, and that can make it harder to speak smoothly.\n\nBut I don’t think my thoughts are in pieces, or that I can’t put words together. I can usually explain myself if I slow down. It’s more like there’s an extra layer going on in my head while I’m trying to talk, and that makes me hesitate or go around the point too much. I might pause, or repeat myself, or lose confidence in what I was saying for a moment.\n\nSo… difficult, yes, in the sense of being distracted or unsure. But not completely jumbled, I don’t think. At least, I haven’t experienced it as my mind breaking apart into disconnected bits.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to a topic when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way, anyway. I mean, I can be a bit long-winded — my colleagues would probably say I explain things with too much background, especially when I’m trying to be precise — but that’s not new for me. I’ve always been the sort of person who gives the context before the answer.\n\nSometimes I do notice myself circling around a point more than I’d like, or taking a detour because I’m trying to make sure I’m being understood properly. But if someone says, “Sorry, what were you saying?” I can usually get back to the topic. I don’t feel as though I’m constantly jumping from subject to subject without realizing it.\n\nSo… I suppose I may wander a little when I’m anxious or trying to explain something complicated, but I wouldn’t say I often have trouble sticking to the topic in conversation.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences, such as hearing voices or feeling like messages are coded, caused problems in your relationships with colleagues, students, or family members?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, like everything has fallen apart, but yes, it has caused problems. I’m still teaching, I should say that first — I haven’t stopped working, and I’m still getting through my classes. But I’m not functioning quite the way I normally would.\n\nWith colleagues, I’ve had to lean on people more than I like. There have been project lessons where I just couldn’t manage all the moving parts properly, and coworkers had to cover parts of it or step in more than they usually would. And in planning meetings, I sometimes need help getting through the agenda or keeping things organized enough to be useful. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been someone people could depend on for that kind of thing.\n\nWith students, I try very hard not to let it show. I don’t think I’ve been… harmful or anything like that. But I know I’m less steady than I used to be, especially with the more open-ended lessons where there are lots of materials, instructions, and questions all happening at once.\n\nAnd outside school, I’ve become unreliable. I’ve missed book club, theater plans, neighborhood things — not because I don’t care, but because I keep pulling back or cancelling, or I just don’t have the confidence that I’ll be able to be present and normal through the whole thing. So yes, it’s affected relationships, mostly by making me less dependable than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that don't make sense to you or seem disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… or at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. My thoughts can feel crowded, and I can get stuck trying to interpret something or worrying about whether I’ve understood it properly, but they don’t usually feel completely disconnected from each other.\n\nIt’s more like I follow a thread too far, if that makes sense. One idea leads to another and then another, and by the time I’m explaining it out loud it may sound overcomplicated, because I’m giving all the steps in between. But in my own head there’s usually a reason I got from one point to the next. It may not be a good reason, or it may seem excessive when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel random to me.\n\nSometimes afterward I might think, “Why did I spend so long on that?” or “That probably didn’t need five minutes of explanation.” But I wouldn’t say I’m having thoughts that don’t make sense to me, exactly. More that I get tangled in them, or I overthink them, and then I have trouble being concise.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having trouble understanding your own thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I understand what I’m feeling most of the time — anxious, embarrassed, tired, frustrated with myself — those things are fairly clear to me. It’s not like I’m sitting there unable to name whether I’m sad or angry or frightened.\n\nWhere I do get muddled is more in… sorting out why I’m feeling that way, or how much weight to give it. For example, I might feel uneasy and then spend too long trying to work out whether there’s a real reason for it, or whether I’m just overreacting, or whether I’ve misunderstood something. So the feeling itself is recognizable, but then I start analyzing it and making it more complicated than it probably needs to be.\n\nWith thoughts, it’s similar. I don’t usually feel as if my own thoughts are impossible to understand. They can be busy, and I can get tangled up in them, but I can generally follow what I mean. Sometimes I wish I could step back from them more cleanly, because I’ll go round and round trying to explain something to myself. But I wouldn’t say I often don’t understand my own thoughts or feelings. More that I over-examine them until they feel less clear than they were at the start.\n\nDoctor: Do these experiences, such as hearing voices or feeling like messages are coded, happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It isn’t constant, like every minute of the day, but it has been going on for a while now — about four months, I think, if I’m being honest about the timing.\n\nThe coded-message feeling is the one that tends to come up in patches. I’ll be going along normally enough, and then something will catch my attention and I’ll start wondering if there’s more meaning in it than there should be. Some days it’s barely there, and other days I can lose quite a lot of time turning it over in my head.\n\nAnd the voice… that’s not all the time either. It happens sometimes, and sometimes I answer it, which I know sounds strange when I say it out loud. But again, it’s been part of this same stretch of time — roughly four months — not something that’s been lifelong or happening every single moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble putting your thoughts into words when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: Not exactly trouble finding the words, no. I can usually put a sentence together — sometimes too many sentences, honestly. I know I go on and explain around things, especially if I’m worried I haven’t been clear.\n\nBut I have noticed something different, which may be related or may not be what you’re asking. For many months now, it’s felt like the… the warmth has gone out of me a bit. Not that I don’t care. I do care about my students, and I care about my friends. If one of them were upset or needed something, I’d still feel responsible, I’d still want them to be all right. But the drive to reach out, to take part, to keep things going socially — that has really faded.\n\nI used to be the sort of person who would help organize things, like book club, or planning a theater outing, or just ringing someone because I hadn’t heard from them. And now I let those things slide unless someone gives me a nudge or practically puts it in front of me. Even simple phone calls feel like something I keep postponing, not because I dislike the person, but because I can’t seem to summon the energy or the feeling behind it.\n\nSo with speaking, if I’m already talking to someone, the words are there eventually. But starting the conversation, keeping up that ordinary back-and-forth, showing the same warmth I used to — that’s harder than it was. It makes me feel guilty, because from the outside it probably looks like I’ve become indifferent, and I don’t think that’s true. It’s more like something has drained out of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself rambling or having trouble staying on topic when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I ramble in the sense of not making sense or completely losing the thread. I mean… I’m aware I can be long-winded. You’ve probably noticed that already. I tend to explain the background, then the background to the background, because I don’t want to give the wrong impression or leave out something important.\n\nBut if you asked me whether I’m unaware that I’ve gone off topic, I don’t think so. Usually I know what point I’m trying to get to, even if I take a rather scenic route to get there. Sometimes people look a bit impatient, and then I realize I’ve been circling something instead of just saying it plainly. That’s embarrassing, especially at work, because I’m used to needing to be clear.\n\nSo yes, I can talk too much around a subject, particularly when I’m nervous or trying to be precise. But I wouldn’t say I often lose the topic entirely. It’s more that I over-explain.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that others often have difficulty understanding what you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not often. At least, people don’t usually say they can’t understand me. If anything, I sometimes worry I’m saying too much, or taking too long to get to the point, but I usually feel that the point is still there somewhere.\n\nWith my students, for example, I’m used to checking whether I’m being clear, because that’s part of teaching — you can see it in their faces when you’ve lost them. And I wouldn’t say that’s been a major problem in the sense of my words not making sense. I might explain something in two or three different ways when one would have done, but that’s not quite the same as being unintelligible.\n\nIn conversations with friends, I suppose they might get tired or want me to be more direct, but I don’t think they’re confused by what I mean. Or if they are, they haven’t told me plainly. I’m more self-conscious about going on too long than about not being understood.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It does vary, yes. It isn’t at exactly the same level all the time. Some days it’s more like a background worry — I can tell myself, “No, that email went to everyone, it’s probably just a normal staff email,” and I can get on with the day, even if it still nags at me a bit.

But other times it feels much stronger, especially with things like staff emails or the morning announcements at school. I’ll read a perfectly ordinary message, or hear something over the tannoy, and there’s this immediate sense that it’s not just general information — that there’s some coded meaning in it about me. Not necessarily obvious to everyone else, but… directed somehow. And then I can spend far too long going back over the wording, thinking, “Why did they say it that way? Was that phrase chosen because of something I did?”

My colleagues have been kind about it, really. A couple of times they’ve shown me, quite gently, that the email was sent to everyone, or that the announcement was just routine. And I can see that, on one level. I’m not saying I have proof. But the feeling doesn’t always go away just because someone shows me the practical explanation.

So, yes, it comes and goes in severity. At its milder moments I can almost step back from it. At other times I can’t shake it, and that’s really what made me come in.

Like they’re coded messages about me.\n\nI’ll read an email that’s been sent to everyone, or hear an announcement over the speakers, and there’ll be a phrase or a timing or just the way something is worded, and it feels very pointed, like it’s referring to me or something I’ve done. My colleagues have been kind about it — they’ll show me, “Look, this went to the whole staff,” or explain that the announcement was general — and I can see that, I really can. But the feeling doesn’t go away easily. It stays with me, and I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nI came because it’s starting to bother me, I guess. I don’t want to keep misreading things at work, but it feels very real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that others can't hear, commenting on you or your actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’ve been hesitant to say that because it sounds worse when I put it into words. But yes, sometimes I do.\n\nIt’s not constant or anything. It’s more when things are quiet — like at home in the evening, when the house has settled, or sometimes after the classroom empties and there’s that strange silence after a whole day of noise. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, quite clearly. Not like a vague thought, more like someone has just spoken from nearby. And sometimes it’ll be a short comment about what I’m doing, like just a few words, nothing long.\n\nA couple of times I’ve actually answered before I caught myself, which was embarrassing even though no one was there to see it. I’ll say, “Yes?” or turn around expecting someone to be standing behind me, and then I realize the room is empty. It unsettles me because in the moment it really does sound external, not like my own inner voice. Then afterward I try to explain it away — tiredness, stress, the acoustics of the building, something from the corridor — but it’s clear enough that I can’t always dismiss it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or have trouble keeping your conversations on track?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I can be long-winded — my students would probably tell you I take the scenic route to get to a point, and my colleagues sometimes tease me that I give too much background before answering a simple question. But that’s not new, really. I’ve always been a bit like that, especially when I’m trying to be precise or explain something properly.\n\nAs far as losing the thread completely, or people not understanding me… I don’t think so. At school I can still teach a lesson and follow what I’m saying. I might circle around a point if I’m anxious or if I’m trying to make sense of something, but I wouldn’t say my thoughts are impossible to express. At least, I haven’t had anyone tell me that I’m not making sense.\n\nUnless you mean something more subtle? Sometimes when I’m under pressure I feel like there are too many thoughts at once, and I have to slow myself down, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or daily activities that seem unusual or out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. It’s not dramatic, exactly, but it’s out of character enough that I’ve noticed it, and then I feel foolish afterward.\n\nFor example, during lessons, I’ll be in the middle of doing something perfectly ordinary — handing out worksheets, setting up an activity, explaining something on the board — and I’ll suddenly get caught on the markers or the papers on my desk. I’ll stop and start arranging them in a very particular way. Not just tidying, I mean more like… the blue marker has to be beside the green one, the papers have to be squared off, or lined up in a certain pattern, and I’ll spend too long on it while the students are waiting. Then I sort of come back to myself and realize I’ve interrupted the flow of the lesson.\n\nIt’s happened in art-fair meetings too. We’ll be discussing displays or budgets or where things should go, and instead of just answering someone, I might repeat a phrase they’ve just said. Like they’ll say, “We need to keep it simple,” and I’ll say, “Keep it simple, yes, keep it simple,” almost automatically, rather than giving a proper response. And then there’s this awkward pause, because I know it sounds strange, and I feel embarrassed. I usually try to cover it by turning it into a joke or pretending I was thinking aloud, but it doesn’t feel quite normal for me.\n\nIt’s the interruption that bothers me most. I can still do my work, but these little moments pull me off track, and I’m aware afterward that it wasn’t how I would usually behave.\n\nDoctor: How long do these feelings of coded messages and hearing voices typically last after they start?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I think. Not every day, and not always in the same way, which makes it harder to pin down neatly, but if I’m being honest, that’s roughly when I first started noticing both things — the feeling that certain things were sort of coded or meant for me, and the voice that I sometimes respond to before I realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAt first I tried to tell myself it was just a bad week, or being overtired, or my mind making patterns because I was stressed. So I didn’t mark it down like, “This began on this date.” But looking back, it’s been around four months that it’s been happening enough for me to feel unsettled by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often fragmented or difficult to put into words when these feelings of coded messages or hearing voices are happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as fragmented, exactly. When it’s happening, it’s more that I become very preoccupied, if that makes sense. My attention gets pulled toward whatever it is, and then I’m trying to work out what it means or whether I’m imagining it, and that can make it harder to speak smoothly.\n\nBut I don’t think my thoughts are in pieces, or that I can’t put words together. I can usually explain myself if I slow down. It’s more like there’s an extra layer going on in my head while I’m trying to talk, and that makes me hesitate or go around the point too much. I might pause, or repeat myself, or lose confidence in what I was saying for a moment.\n\nSo… difficult, yes, in the sense of being distracted or unsure. But not completely jumbled, I don’t think. At least, I haven’t experienced it as my mind breaking apart into disconnected bits.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to a topic when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way, anyway. I mean, I can be a bit long-winded — my colleagues would probably say I explain things with too much background, especially when I’m trying to be precise — but that’s not new for me. I’ve always been the sort of person who gives the context before the answer.\n\nSometimes I do notice myself circling around a point more than I’d like, or taking a detour because I’m trying to make sure I’m being understood properly. But if someone says, “Sorry, what were you saying?” I can usually get back to the topic. I don’t feel as though I’m constantly jumping from subject to subject without realizing it.\n\nSo… I suppose I may wander a little when I’m anxious or trying to explain something complicated, but I wouldn’t say I often have trouble sticking to the topic in conversation.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences, such as hearing voices or feeling like messages are coded, caused problems in your relationships with colleagues, students, or family members?\n\nPatient: Yes… not in a dramatic way, like everything has fallen apart, but yes, it has caused problems. I’m still teaching, I should say that first — I haven’t stopped working, and I’m still getting through my classes. But I’m not functioning quite the way I normally would.\n\nWith colleagues, I’ve had to lean on people more than I like. There have been project lessons where I just couldn’t manage all the moving parts properly, and coworkers had to cover parts of it or step in more than they usually would. And in planning meetings, I sometimes need help getting through the agenda or keeping things organized enough to be useful. That’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been someone people could depend on for that kind of thing.\n\nWith students, I try very hard not to let it show. I don’t think I’ve been… harmful or anything like that. But I know I’m less steady than I used to be, especially with the more open-ended lessons where there are lots of materials, instructions, and questions all happening at once.\n\nAnd outside school, I’ve become unreliable. I’ve missed book club, theater plans, neighborhood things — not because I don’t care, but because I keep pulling back or cancelling, or I just don’t have the confidence that I’ll be able to be present and normal through the whole thing. So yes, it’s affected relationships, mostly by making me less dependable than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having thoughts that don't make sense to you or seem disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… or at least not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. My thoughts can feel crowded, and I can get stuck trying to interpret something or worrying about whether I’ve understood it properly, but they don’t usually feel completely disconnected from each other.\n\nIt’s more like I follow a thread too far, if that makes sense. One idea leads to another and then another, and by the time I’m explaining it out loud it may sound overcomplicated, because I’m giving all the steps in between. But in my own head there’s usually a reason I got from one point to the next. It may not be a good reason, or it may seem excessive when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel random to me.\n\nSometimes afterward I might think, “Why did I spend so long on that?” or “That probably didn’t need five minutes of explanation.” But I wouldn’t say I’m having thoughts that don’t make sense to me, exactly. More that I get tangled in them, or I overthink them, and then I have trouble being concise.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having trouble understanding your own thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I understand what I’m feeling most of the time — anxious, embarrassed, tired, frustrated with myself — those things are fairly clear to me. It’s not like I’m sitting there unable to name whether I’m sad or angry or frightened.\n\nWhere I do get muddled is more in… sorting out why I’m feeling that way, or how much weight to give it. For example, I might feel uneasy and then spend too long trying to work out whether there’s a real reason for it, or whether I’m just overreacting, or whether I’ve misunderstood something. So the feeling itself is recognizable, but then I start analyzing it and making it more complicated than it probably needs to be.\n\nWith thoughts, it’s similar. I don’t usually feel as if my own thoughts are impossible to understand. They can be busy, and I can get tangled up in them, but I can generally follow what I mean. Sometimes I wish I could step back from them more cleanly, because I’ll go round and round trying to explain something to myself. But I wouldn’t say I often don’t understand my own thoughts or feelings. More that I over-examine them until they feel less clear than they were at the start.\n\nDoctor: Do these experiences, such as hearing voices or feeling like messages are coded, happen all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It isn’t constant, like every minute of the day, but it has been going on for a while now — about four months, I think, if I’m being honest about the timing.\n\nThe coded-message feeling is the one that tends to come up in patches. I’ll be going along normally enough, and then something will catch my attention and I’ll start wondering if there’s more meaning in it than there should be. Some days it’s barely there, and other days I can lose quite a lot of time turning it over in my head.\n\nAnd the voice… that’s not all the time either. It happens sometimes, and sometimes I answer it, which I know sounds strange when I say it out loud. But again, it’s been part of this same stretch of time — roughly four months — not something that’s been lifelong or happening every single moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble putting your thoughts into words when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: Not exactly trouble finding the words, no. I can usually put a sentence together — sometimes too many sentences, honestly. I know I go on and explain around things, especially if I’m worried I haven’t been clear.\n\nBut I have noticed something different, which may be related or may not be what you’re asking. For many months now, it’s felt like the… the warmth has gone out of me a bit. Not that I don’t care. I do care about my students, and I care about my friends. If one of them were upset or needed something, I’d still feel responsible, I’d still want them to be all right. But the drive to reach out, to take part, to keep things going socially — that has really faded.\n\nI used to be the sort of person who would help organize things, like book club, or planning a theater outing, or just ringing someone because I hadn’t heard from them. And now I let those things slide unless someone gives me a nudge or practically puts it in front of me. Even simple phone calls feel like something I keep postponing, not because I dislike the person, but because I can’t seem to summon the energy or the feeling behind it.\n\nSo with speaking, if I’m already talking to someone, the words are there eventually. But starting the conversation, keeping up that ordinary back-and-forth, showing the same warmth I used to — that’s harder than it was. It makes me feel guilty, because from the outside it probably looks like I’ve become indifferent, and I don’t think that’s true. It’s more like something has drained out of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself rambling or having trouble staying on topic when speaking to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I ramble in the sense of not making sense or completely losing the thread. I mean… I’m aware I can be long-winded. You’ve probably noticed that already. I tend to explain the background, then the background to the background, because I don’t want to give the wrong impression or leave out something important.\n\nBut if you asked me whether I’m unaware that I’ve gone off topic, I don’t think so. Usually I know what point I’m trying to get to, even if I take a rather scenic route to get there. Sometimes people look a bit impatient, and then I realize I’ve been circling something instead of just saying it plainly. That’s embarrassing, especially at work, because I’m used to needing to be clear.\n\nSo yes, I can talk too much around a subject, particularly when I’m nervous or trying to be precise. But I wouldn’t say I often lose the topic entirely. It’s more that I over-explain.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that others often have difficulty understanding what you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not often. At least, people don’t usually say they can’t understand me. If anything, I sometimes worry I’m saying too much, or taking too long to get to the point, but I usually feel that the point is still there somewhere.\n\nWith my students, for example, I’m used to checking whether I’m being clear, because that’s part of teaching — you can see it in their faces when you’ve lost them. And I wouldn’t say that’s been a major problem in the sense of my words not making sense. I might explain something in two or three different ways when one would have done, but that’s not quite the same as being unintelligible.\n\nIn conversations with friends, I suppose they might get tired or want me to be more direct, but I don’t think they’re confused by what I mean. Or if they are, they haven’t told me plainly. I’m more self-conscious about going on too long than about not being understood.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these feelings of coded messages and hearing voices at the same level of intensity all the time, or do they vary in severity?\n\nPatient: It does vary, yes. It isn’t at exactly the same level all the time. Some days it’s more like a background worry — I can tell myself, “No, that email went to everyone, it’s probably just a normal staff email,” and I can get on with the day, even if it still nags at me a bit.\n\nBut other times it feels much stronger, especially with things like staff emails or the morning announcements at school. I’ll read a perfectly ordinary message, or hear something over the tannoy, and there’s this immediate sense that it’s not just general information — that there’s some coded meaning in it about me. Not necessarily obvious to everyone else, but… directed somehow. And then I can spend far too long going back over the wording, thinking, “Why did they say it that way? Was that phrase chosen because of something I did?”\n\nMy colleagues have been kind about it, really. A couple of times they’ve shown me, quite gently, that the email was sent to everyone, or that the announcement was just routine. And I can see that, on one level. I’m not saying I have proof. But the feeling doesn’t always go away just because someone shows me the practical explanation.\n\nSo, yes, it comes and goes in severity. At its milder moments I can almost step back from it. At other times I can’t shake it, and that’s really what made me come in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
